On the Itô-Stratonovich Diffusion Limit for the Magnetic Field in a 3D Thin Domain
Federico Butori, Franco Flandoli, Eliseo Luongo

Abstract
. We introduce a stochastic model for a passive magnetic field in a three dimensional thin domain. The velocity field, white in time and modelling phenomenologically a turbulent fluid, acts on the magnetic field as a transport-stretching noise. We prove, in a quantitative way, that, in the simultaneous scaling limit of the thickness of the thin layer and the separation of scales, the mean on the thin direction of the magnetic field is close to the solution of the equation of the magnetic field with additional dissipation. In certain choice of noises with correlation between their components, without mirror symmetry and with a non zero mean helicity, we identify an alpha-term, in addition to the extra dissipation term. However, it does not produce dynamo; consequently, we extend a no-dynamo theorem to thin layers.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
60H15, 76F25

1.
Introduction

We consider a linear vector-valued SPDE in the unknown
B_{t}^{\varepsilon}=B_{t}^{\varepsilon}\left(x\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
x\in\mathbb{T}_{\varepsilon}^{3}=\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}/(2\varepsilon\pi\mathbb{Z})
(a model of thin domain),
t\in\left[0,T\right]
, of
advection type, loosely written as (see the rigorous formulation in
subsection 2.4
below)

(1.1)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}B_{t}^{\varepsilon}+\nabla\times\left(v_{t}^{\varepsilon}\times B_{t}^{\varepsilon}\right)&=\eta\Delta B_{t}^{\varepsilon}\\
\operatorname{div}B_{t}^{\varepsilon}&=0\\
B_{t}^{\varepsilon}|_{t=0}&=B_{0}^{\varepsilon}\end{cases}

where
v_{t}^{\varepsilon}=v_{t}^{\varepsilon}\left(x\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
is a white noise in time, with suitable space covariance
structure, described in detail in
subsection 2.3
. The multiplications

v_{t}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla B_{t}^{\varepsilon}
and
B_{t}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla v_{t}^{\varepsilon}
appearing in the development of the term

\nabla\times\left(v_{t}^{\varepsilon}\times B_{t}^{\varepsilon}\right)

are understood in Stratonovich sense; then the system is rewritten, as
explained in
subsection 2.4
, as an Itô system with the Itô-Stratonovich corrector.

We prove that the vertical average
\overline{B^{\varepsilon}_{t}}=M_{\varepsilon}B_{t}^{\varepsilon}
,
M_{\varepsilon}
given by
2.2
below, converges in a suitable distributional topology to the solution

\overline{B}_{t}
of the system

(1.2)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\overline{B}_{t}&=\left(\eta+\eta_{T}\right)\Delta\overline{B}_{t}+\nabla\times\left(\mathcal{A}\overline{B}_{t}\right)\\
\operatorname{div}\overline{B}_{t}&=0\\
\overline{B}_{t}|_{t=0}&=\overline{B}_{0}\end{cases}

where
\overline{B}_{0}
is the limit of
M_{\varepsilon}B_{0}^{\varepsilon}
.
Here
\eta_{T}
is an “eddy magnetic diffusivity”, linked to the
space-covariance of
v_{t}^{\varepsilon}\left(x\right)
; the additional
term
\eta_{T}\Delta\overline{B}_{t}
is often called the beta-term in the
literature devoted to equations for the magnetic field driven by a turbulent
velocity field (see
subsection 1.1
below).
\mathcal{A}
is
a matrix; the term
\nabla\times\left(\mathcal{A}\overline{B}_{t}\right)

is usually called the alpha-term. The convergence result is stated in detail
by
Theorem 2.15
and the formulation given above is reconstructed in
Appendix B
.

We classify such a theorem as
Itô-Stratonovich stochastic
diffusion limit
, in analogy with the deterministic and stochastic theory of
diffusion limits based on homogenization techniques (see for instance

[
16
]
,
[
13
]
). The approach however is different, strongly
based on the Itô-Stratonovich corrector, initiated by
[
11
]
(see also

[
9
]
and references therein), from which we give this name.

Theorem 2.15
, in the particular geometry of the thin domain is new and
particularly challenging both from the viewpoint of the proof (we devote

subsection 2.5
to explain the strategy and its difficulties) and the Physics
behind (see
subsection 1.1
). The closest previous result in
this direction is
[
6
]
, a 2D-2C (two-dimensional, three component)
model, where a number of ideas used also here have been already introduced.
However, the thin domain treated here is a truly 3D-3C model, which reduces to
2D-2C only in the limit. The control of the limit is demanding, especially for
the noise part, the new one compared to the classical references on thin
domain equations. This is the first truly 3D problem where the technique of
Itô-Stratonovich stochastic diffusion limit is developed. The main source
of difficulty is the stochastic stretching term
B_{t}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla v_{t}^{\varepsilon}
; without it, as in
[
5
]
, the
control of the limit is easier. The 2D-3C problem of
[
6
]
, being 2D
from the beginning, eliminates part of the difficulty. The treatment of the
stretching term in full generality, namely not in the particular case of thin
domains and under our assumption described in
subsection 2.3
, is an open problem.

The content of the paper is the following one. In

subsection 1.1
we describe some ideas that motivated us to treat this
problem. In
section 2
we introduce rigorously our framework. In particular, we define the structure of the noise,
v^{\varepsilon}_{t}
, in
subsection 2.3
, while we state our main result in
subsection 2.4
and describe the main ideas behind its proof in
subsection 2.5
. The remainder of the paper is dedicated to proving our main result. In
section 3
we prove estimates for
B^{\varepsilon}_{t}
uniformly in the thickness of the domain, then we show the convergence of
\overline{B^{\varepsilon}_{t}}
to
\overline{B}_{t}
in
section 4
. We conclude the paper with two appendices. In
Appendix A
we provide an explicit representation of the Itô-Stratonovich corrector in (
1.1
). In
Appendix B
we compute an average of the helicity of
v^{\varepsilon}_{t}
, highlighting its connection to the alpha-term in (
1.2
).

1.1.
Comments on the Physical motivation

The model introduced above for
B_{t}^{\varepsilon}
is a classical model for
a magnetic field driven by a velocity field
v_{t}^{\varepsilon}
, with some
degree of resistivity (namely closedness to ideal) given by
\frac{1}{\eta}
. We refer to the classical monographs
[
18
]
,
[
19
]
for a detailed discussion on the topics highlighted in this section.
This model is used in Plasma Physics to investigate several phenomena, like
reconnection and dynamo effect. We shall deal with reconnection in a future
work under the more specific geometry of toroidal plasma. Here let us comment
about the model and result under the usual view of dynamo research.

The step from the equation for
B_{t}^{\varepsilon}
to a “mean field” equation like the one above for
\overline{B}_{t}
is a classical
step in the Physics literature on this topic. The usual procedure consists in
taking averages of
B_{t}^{\varepsilon}
, getting a Reynolds type tensor which
mixes bilinearly the randomness of
B_{t}^{\varepsilon}
and
v_{t}^{\varepsilon}
, and close the equation for the average of
B_{t}^{\varepsilon}
by suitable closure assumptions on the average of the bilinear
term. Compared to these derivations, we do not aim to get new final mean field
equations, since those indicated in Plasma Physics literature are already deep
and compared with reality; our aim is to justify them. Indeed, their validity
is far from trivial, presumably false in full generality, for substantial
reasons and not just for a question of mathematical rigour. Let us explain why.

In our approach, the Itô-Stratonovich corrector appearing when we rewrite
the term
\nabla\times\left(v_{t}^{\varepsilon}\times B_{t}^{\varepsilon}\right)
from the Stratonovich form to the Itô one, corresponds
precisely to the mean field term (or sum of terms) discovered in the
literature under the most natural closure formulae. But in our rigorous
derivation there is still the Itô term, which should disappear in the
limit when
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
. There is no obvious reason why it should
disappear, and in general it contains fluctuations which may be very important
and alter the limit result. In scalar and 2D models, where stretching of
vector quantities does not appear, it is possible to prove that the Itô
term is negligible in the limit (see for instance
[
11
]
,
[
9
]
,
[
4
]
,
[
3
]
,
[
1
]
,
[
7
]
,
[
10
]
). In 3D, the problem is extremely difficult. We start to throw some light
with this work in a truly 3D model, thanks to the asymptotic simplifications
of the thin domain; previously some basic ingredients have been understood for
the 2D-3C model of
[
6
]
.

Therefore, to summarize the previous discussion, our aim is making rigorous,
when true, the mean field theory of a magnetic field
B_{t}^{\varepsilon}

advected by a turbulent velocity field
v_{t}^{\varepsilon}
. We have
identified here a framework where the mean field theory is correct.

Let us now come to some of the usual conclusions of the (previously heuristic)
mean field theory and let us compare our results with them. The final mean
field system for
\overline{B}_{t}
contains two new terms, the alpha and beta
terms. Let us start from the beta term
\eta_{T}\Delta\overline{B}_{t}
. Let
us recall the following sentence from Krause e Rädler
[
14
, Page 12]
:
“homogeneous isotropic mirror symmetric turbulence only
influences the decay rate of the mean magnetic fields, which is enhanced in
almost all cases of physical interest”. When the field

v_{t}^{\varepsilon}
is mirror symmetric, the average elicity of

v_{t}^{\varepsilon}
(defined in
Appendix B
below in the case of our white
noise field
v_{t}^{\varepsilon}
) is zero, which then implies that

\mathcal{A}=0
, namely the alpha-term
\nabla\times\left(\mathcal{A}\overline{B}_{t}\right)
is absent. In this case, the only mean-field effect
of the turbulent field
v_{t}^{\varepsilon}
is to produce the beta-term

\eta_{T}\Delta\overline{B}_{t}
, which enhances the decay rate, as stated by
[
14
, Page 12]
. Therefore our result rigorously confirms that prediction, in
the particular case of thin domains.

Concerning the other additional term, namely the alpha-term
\nabla\times\left(\mathcal{A}\overline{B}_{t}\right)
, the story is more
complicated. In fully 3D cases such a term may be responsible for the growth
of the magnetic field by stretching and it is advocated as one of the sources
of dynamo, a very important observed phenomenon which is related to the
maintenance of the magnetic field in certain planets and stars. However, in
our case the term
\nabla\times\left(\mathcal{A}\overline{B}_{t}\right)
,
although present, cannot produce an increase of the magnetic field. This is
due to the specific algebraic structure, described in
subsection 2.4
. The matrix

\mathcal{A}
is degenerate and propagates only certain information of

\overline{B}_{t}
(those coming from the vertical part of the vector
potential) which do not create a loop suitable for increase (the vertical part
of the vector potential may only dissipate). Therefore, the conclusion of our
work is that in the thin domain geometry we may produce an alpha-term but not
a dynamo. This is coherent with conclusions of the Plasma Physics literature
which state that dynamo does not appear when the magnetic field depends only
on two variables, see for instance
[
12
, Section 3.5]
. This happens only in the limit of the thin domain, not for
the approximating equation, but the final lack of dynamo remains true.

2.
Functional Setting and Main Results

In
subsection 2.1
we introduce the notation we follow along the paper. In
subsection 2.2
we introduce some functional analysis tools we employ in order to carry on rigorously our analysis described in
section 1
. We describe the noise we employ in
subsection 2.3
providing some insights in the meaning of the assumptions and comparisons with the existing literature. We state our main result in
subsection 2.4
and describe the main ideas behind its proof in
subsection 2.5
for the convenience of the reader.

2.1.
Notation

In the following we denote by
\mathbb{T}^{2}=\mathbb{R}^{2}/(2\pi\mathbb{Z})^{2}
(resp.
\mathbb{T}^{3}=\mathbb{R}^{2}/(2\pi\mathbb{Z})^{3}
) the two (resp. three) dimensional torus. Moreover, for each
\varepsilon>0
let us denote by

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}^{3}_{\varepsilon}=\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}/(2\varepsilon\pi\mathbb{Z})

the thin layer three dimensional torus. In
subsection 2.2
we focus on function spaces on periodic domains which we in general denote by
\mathcal{D}
varying between
\mathbb{T}^{2},\ \mathbb{T}^{3},\ \mathbb{T}^{3}_{\varepsilon}
.

Similarly we denote by
\mathbb{Z}^{3}_{0}=\mathbb{Z}^{3}\smallsetminus\{0\}
(resp.
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{0}=\mathbb{Z}^{2}\smallsetminus\{0\}
) the three (resp. two) dimensional lattice made by points with integer coordinates. Moreover, for each
\varepsilon>0
let us denote by

\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}^{3,\varepsilon^{-1}}_{0}=\mathbb{Z}^{2}\times\varepsilon^{-1}\mathbb{Z}\smallsetminus\{0\}

the thin layer three dimensional lattice. We introduce a partition of
\mathbb{Z}^{3,\varepsilon^{-1}}_{0}=\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{+}\cup\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{-}
such that

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{+}=\{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,\varepsilon^{-1}}_{0}:k_{3}>0\vee(k_{3}=0\land k_{2}>0)\vee(k_{3}=k_{2}=0\land k_{1}>0)\},\quad\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{-}=-\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{+}.

If
v=(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})^{t}:\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
we will sometimes denote by
Dv=(\nabla v)^{t}
,
v_{H}=(v_{1},v_{2})^{t}
in the following.

For each
j\geq 0,\ l>1
we denote by

\displaystyle\zeta^{N}_{H,j}=N^{j-2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{H}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{0}\\
N\leq\lvert k_{H}\rvert\leq 2N\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\lvert k_{H}\rvert^{j}},\quad\zeta_{H,j}=\int_{1\leq\lvert x\rvert\leq 2}\frac{dx_{1}dx_{2}}{\lvert x\rvert^{j}}=2\pi\begin{cases}\log 2&\text{ if }j=2,\\
\frac{2^{2-j}-1}{2-j}&\text{ if }j\neq 2\end{cases},\quad\zeta_{l}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}\smallsetminus\{0\}}\frac{1}{\lvert k\rvert^{l}}<+\infty.

In particular, by the approximation properties of Riemann sums, for each
j\geq 0
it holds

\displaystyle\zeta_{H,j}^{N}=\zeta_{H,j}N^{2-j}+O(N^{1-j}).

For two vector fields
X
and
Y
, we write
\mathcal{L}_{X}Y
for the Lie derivative
\mathcal{L}_{X}Y=X\cdot\nabla Y-Y\cdot\nabla X.
We denote by
\{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}\}
the canonical basis of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

We denote by
[M,N]
the quadratic covariation process,
which is defined for any couple
M,\ N
of square integrable real semimartingales and we extend it by bilinearity to the analogue complex valued processes.

Let
a,b
be two positive numbers, then we write
a\lesssim b
if there exists a positive constant
C
such that
a\leq Cb
and
a\lesssim_{\xi}b
when we want to highlight the dependence of the constant
C
on a parameter
\xi
. If
x\in\mathbb{C}
we denote by
x^{*}
its complex conjugate. If
\mathcal{X}
is a tensor we denote by
\left\|\mathcal{X}\right\|_{HS}
its Hilbert-Schmidt norm. Lastly, if
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
we denote by
x^{\perp}=(-x_{2},x_{1})^{t}.

2.2.
Preliminaries

2.2.1.
Function Spaces in the Periodic Setting

Let us start setting some classical notation before describing the main contributions of this work. We refer to monographs
[
9
,
17
,
20
,
24
,
25
,
26
,
27
]
for a complete discussion on the topics shortly recalled in this section.

Let
\left(H^{s,p}(\mathcal{D}),\left\|\cdot\right\|_{H^{s,p}(\mathcal{D})}\right),\ s\in\mathbb{R},\ p\in(1,+\infty)
be the Bessel spaces of periodic functions. In case of
p=2
, we simply write
H^{s}(\mathcal{D})
in place of
H^{s,2}(\mathcal{D})
and we denote by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{H^{s}(\mathcal{D})}
the corresponding scalar products. When dealing with functions having
\mathbb{T}^{2}
as a domain we neglect the domain in the definition of norm and inner product, i.e. we write
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{H^{s,p}}
in place of
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{H^{s,p}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}
.
In case of
s=0
, we write
L^{2}(\mathcal{D})
instead of
H^{0}(\mathcal{D})
and we neglect the subscript in the notation for the norm and the inner product when considering functions having
\mathbb{T}^{2}
as a domain. Instead, in case of functions having
\mathbb{T}^{3}_{\varepsilon}
as a domain we write
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{\varepsilon}
(resp.
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\varepsilon}
) in place of
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3}_{\varepsilon})}
(resp.
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3}_{\varepsilon})}
). With some abuse of notation, for
s>0
, we denote the duality between
H^{-s}(\mathcal{D})
and
H^{s}(\mathcal{D})
by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D})}
.

We denote by
\dot{H}^{s,p}(\mathcal{D})
(resp.
\dot{H}^{s}(\mathcal{D}),\ \dot{L^{2}}(\mathcal{D})
) the closed subspace of
H^{s,p}(\mathcal{D})
(resp.
H^{s}(\mathcal{D}),\ L^{2}(\mathcal{D})
) made by zero mean functions with norm inducted by
H^{s,p}(\mathcal{D})
(resp.
H^{s}(\mathcal{D}),\ L^{2}(\mathcal{D})
).
With some abuse of notation we denote the Laplacian with the same symbol,
\Delta
, either when considering functions with domain
\mathbb{T}^{2},\mathbb{T}^{3}
or
\mathbb{T}^{3}_{\varepsilon}
, i.e. we denote by

\displaystyle\Delta:\mathsf{D}(\Delta)\subseteq\dot{L^{2}}(\mathcal{D})\rightarrow\dot{L}^{2}(\mathcal{D}),

where
\mathsf{D}(\Delta)=\dot{H}^{2}(\mathcal{D})
. We follow the same notation when considering the orthogonal projection of
L^{2}(\mathcal{D})
on
\dot{L}^{2}(\mathcal{D})
, which we denote by
Q:L^{2}(\mathcal{D})\rightarrow\dot{L}^{2}(\mathcal{D})
. The restriction (resp. extension) of
Q
to
H^{s}(\mathcal{D})
(resp.
H^{-s}(\mathcal{D})
) for
s>0
is the orthogonal projection on
\dot{H}^{s}(\mathcal{D})
(resp.
\dot{H}^{-s}(\mathcal{D})
). It is well known that
{\Delta}
is the infinitesimal generator of analytic semigroup of negative type that we denote by
e^{t\Delta}:\dot{L}^{2}(\mathcal{D})\rightarrow\dot{L}^{2}(\mathcal{D})
and moreover for each
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
,
\mathsf{D}((-\Delta)^{\alpha})
can be identified with
\dot{H}^{2\alpha}(\mathcal{D})
. It is well known that the family
\{\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{2\pi\varepsilon}}e^{ik\cdot x}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,\varepsilon^{-1}}_{0}}
(resp.
\{\frac{e^{ik\cdot x}}{2\pi}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{0}}
) is a complete orthogonal systems of
\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{3}_{\varepsilon})
(resp.
\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
) made by eigengunctions of
-\Delta
. Moreover it is orthonormal in
\dot{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3}_{\varepsilon})
(resp.
\dot{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
). We recall for the convenience of the reader some properties of the Heat Semigroup we will exploit in the following:

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
q\in\dot{H}^{\alpha}(\mathcal{D}),\ \alpha\in\mathbb{R}
. Then:

i)

for any
\varphi\geq 0,
it holds
\lVert e^{t{\Delta}}q\rVert_{\dot{H}^{\alpha+\varphi}}\leq C_{\varphi}t^{-\varphi/2}\lVert q\rVert_{\dot{H}^{\alpha}}
for some constant increasing in
\varphi
;

ii)

for any
\varphi\in[0,2],
it holds
\lVert\left(I-e^{t{\Delta}}\right)q\rVert_{\dot{H}^{\alpha-\varphi}}\lesssim_{\varphi}t^{\varphi/2}\lVert q\rVert_{\dot{H}^{\alpha}}
.

Similarly, for
d\in\{2,3\}
we introduce the Bessel spaces of zero mean vector fields

\displaystyle{H}^{s,p}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{d})

\displaystyle=\{u=(u_{1},\dots,u_{d})^{t}:\ u_{1},\dots,u_{d}\in H^{s,p}(\mathcal{D})\},

\displaystyle\langle u,v\rangle_{{H}^{s}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\langle u_{j},v_{j}\rangle_{H^{s}},\quad\text{for }s\in\mathbb{R}.

Again, in case of
s=0
we write
{L}^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{d})
instead of
{H}^{0}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{d})
and we neglect the subscript in the notation for the norm and the scalar product. Similarly we denote by
\dot{H}^{s}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{d})
(resp.
\dot{L}^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{d})
) the closed subspace of
H^{s}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{d})
(resp.
L^{2}(\mathcal{D};\mathbb{R}^{d})
) made by zero mean functions. In case of
\mathcal{D}=\mathbb{T}^{3}_{\varepsilon},\ d=3
we denote by
\mathbf{H}_{\varepsilon}^{s}
(resp.
\mathbf{L}^{2}_{\varepsilon}
) the closed subspace of
H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{3}_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{3})
(resp.
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3}_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{3})
) made by zero mean, divergence free vector fields with norm induces by
H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{3}_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{3})
(resp.
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3}_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{3})
).
With some abuse of notation we usually employ the same notation for norms and inner products that we introduced for scalar functions also for the corresponding vector fields, forgetting their range and we denote by

\displaystyle(-\Delta)^{\alpha}f=((-\Delta)^{\alpha}f_{1},\dots,(-\Delta)^{\alpha}f_{i})^{t}\quad\text{for }f\in\dot{H}^{2\alpha}(\mathcal{D};{\mathbb{R}}^{d}).

Having this notation in mind we recall the definition and the regularizing property of the Leray Projection and the Biot-Savart operator. We denote by

\displaystyle P_{\varepsilon}:\dot{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3}_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{3})\rightarrow\mathbf{L}^{2}_{\varepsilon}

the Leray projection which, in the periodic setting, is an orthogonal projection even when acting between
\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{3}_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{3})
and
\mathbf{H}^{s}_{\varepsilon}.

If
v\in\mathbf{H}_{\varepsilon}^{s}
there exists a unique vector field
K_{\varepsilon}[v]
such that
\operatorname{curl}K_{\varepsilon}[v]=v,\ \operatorname{div}K_{\varepsilon}[v]=0
. Moreover the operator

\displaystyle K_{\varepsilon}:\mathbf{H}_{\varepsilon}^{s}\rightarrow\mathbf{H}_{\varepsilon}^{s+1}

is linear and continuous for each
s\in\mathbb{R}
and is given by
K_{\varepsilon}[v]=(-\Delta)^{-1}\operatorname{curl}v
. In particular, if
v:\mathbb{T}^{3}_{\varepsilon}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3},\ v=(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})^{t},
is independent from the third component, we have

(2.1)

\displaystyle K_{\varepsilon}[v]=(-\nabla_{H}^{\perp}(-\Delta)^{-1}v^{3},(-\Delta)^{-1}\nabla^{\perp}_{H}\cdot v^{H})^{t},

where we denoted by
\nabla^{\perp}_{H}=(-\partial_{2},\partial_{1})
.

We conclude this subsection introducing some classical notation when dealing with stochastic processes taking values in separable Hilbert spaces. Let
Z
be a separable Hilbert space, with associated norm
\|\cdot\|_{Z}
. We denote by
C_{\mathcal{F}}\left(\left[0,T\right];Z\right)
the space of weakly continuous adapted processes
\left(X_{t}\right)_{t\in\left[0,T\right]}
with values in
Z
such that

\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sup_{t\in\left[0,T\right]}\left\|X_{t}\right\|_{Z}^{2}\bigg]<\infty

and by
L_{\mathcal{F}}^{p}\left(0,T;Z\right),\ p\in[1,\infty),
the space of progressively
measurable processes
\left(X_{t}\right)_{t\in\left[0,T\right]}
with
values in
Z
such that

\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_{0}^{T}\left\|X_{t}\right\|_{Z}^{p}dt\bigg]<\infty.

2.2.2.
Preliminaries on the Thin Layer

As discussed in
section 1
, it is convenient to introduce the mean operator,
M_{\varepsilon}
, and the fluctuations operator,
N_{\varepsilon}
, in order to exploit the properties of working with a thin layer. Therefore, let us recall their definitions and some properties which we will use in the following. We refer to
[
21
,
22
,
23
]
for the proof of these results and some more discussions on the topic.

Definition 2.2
.

We denote by
M_{\varepsilon}:\dot{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3}_{\varepsilon})\rightarrow\dot{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3}_{\varepsilon})
the mean operator in the third variable, i.e.

\displaystyle M_{\varepsilon}\phi(x_{1},x_{2})=\frac{1}{2\pi\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{2\pi\varepsilon}\phi(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})dx_{3}.

Similarly
N_{\varepsilon}=I-M_{\varepsilon}
, therefore
\int_{0}^{2\pi\varepsilon}N_{\varepsilon}\phi(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})dx_{3}=0.

The operator
M_{\varepsilon}
and
N_{\varepsilon}
are projection either in
\dot{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3}_{\varepsilon})
and in
\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{3}_{\varepsilon})
. More in general the following holds.

Lemma 2.3
.

i)

The operators
M_{\varepsilon},\ N_{\varepsilon}
are orthogonal projections in
\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{3}_{\varepsilon})
for each
s\in\mathbb{R}.
In particular:

(2.2)

\displaystyle M_{\varepsilon}N_{\varepsilon}f=N_{\varepsilon}M_{\varepsilon}f=0\quad\forall f\in\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{3}_{\varepsilon}).

ii)

M_{\varepsilon},\ N_{\varepsilon}
commute with derivative and fractional powers of the Laplacian. In particular,
M_{\varepsilon},\ N_{\varepsilon}
commute with
K_{\varepsilon}
.

iii)

For each
u,\ v,\ w:\mathbb{T}^{3}_{\varepsilon}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
such that
\operatorname{div}u=\operatorname{div}v=\operatorname{div}w=0
and
\langle u\cdot\nabla v,w\rangle_{\varepsilon}
is well defined, than we have

(2.3)

\displaystyle\langle u\cdot\nabla v,M_{\varepsilon}w\rangle_{\varepsilon}=\langle M_{\varepsilon}u\cdot\nabla M_{\varepsilon}v,M_{\varepsilon}w\rangle_{\varepsilon}+\langle N_{\varepsilon}u\cdot\nabla N_{\varepsilon}v,M_{\varepsilon}w\rangle_{\varepsilon}.

(2.4)

\displaystyle\langle u\cdot\nabla v,N_{\varepsilon}w\rangle_{\varepsilon}=\langle M_{\varepsilon}u\cdot\nabla N_{\varepsilon}v,N_{\varepsilon}w\rangle_{\varepsilon}+\langle N_{\varepsilon}u\cdot\nabla M_{\varepsilon}v,N_{\varepsilon}w\rangle_{\varepsilon}+\langle N_{\varepsilon}u\cdot\nabla N_{\varepsilon}v,N_{\varepsilon}w\rangle_{\varepsilon}.

iv)

For each
f\in\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{3}_{\varepsilon})
the following Poincaré inequality holds

(2.5)

\displaystyle\left\|N_{\varepsilon}v\right\|_{{\varepsilon}}\leq\varepsilon\left\|\partial_{3}N_{\varepsilon}v\right\|_{{\varepsilon}}.

Remark 2.4
.

In our periodic setting, the mean operator
M_{\varepsilon}
preserves Fourier modes which are independent from the third variable. Instead, the fluctuations operator
N_{\varepsilon}
preserves Fourier modes which depend from the third variable, i.e. if
f\in\dot{H}^{s}({\mathbb{T}^{3}_{\varepsilon}})
then

\displaystyle f=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,\varepsilon^{-1}}_{0}}\langle f,e^{-ik\cdot x}\rangle_{\varepsilon}\frac{e^{ik\cdot x}}{2\pi\varepsilon}

and

\displaystyle M_{\varepsilon}f=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,\varepsilon^{-1}}_{0}}\\
k_{3}=0\end{subarray}}\langle f,e^{-ik\cdot x}\rangle_{\varepsilon}\frac{e^{ik\cdot x}}{8\pi\varepsilon}\quad N_{\varepsilon}f=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,\varepsilon^{-1}}_{0}\\
k_{3}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\langle f,e^{-ik\cdot x}\rangle_{\varepsilon}\frac{e^{ik\cdot x}}{8\pi\varepsilon}.

Exploiting Fourier decomposition, one can easily prove that, more in general, if
u,\ v,\ w:\mathbb{T}^{3}_{\varepsilon}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
are regular enough such that
uv\in H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{3}),\ s\in\mathbb{R}
is well defined (e.g.
s=0,\ u,v\in\dot{L}^{4}(\mathbb{T}^{3}_{\varepsilon})
), then we have

\displaystyle M_{\varepsilon}(Q[uv])=Q[M_{\varepsilon}uM_{\varepsilon}v]+M_{\varepsilon}\left(Q[N_{\varepsilon}uN_{\varepsilon}v]\right),\quad N_{\varepsilon}(Q[uv])=M_{\varepsilon}{u}N_{\varepsilon}v+N_{\varepsilon}uM_{\varepsilon}v+N_{\varepsilon}(Q[N_{\varepsilon}uN_{\varepsilon}v]).

2.3.
Description of the Stochastic System

We move now to the description of the noise. We shall define it in a sort of expansion in the Fourier basis. For each
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,\varepsilon^{-1}}_{0},\ j\in\{1,2\}
we denote by
\sigma_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}(x)=\theta_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}a_{k,j}e^{ik\cdot x}
, where
\{\frac{k}{\lvert k\rvert},a_{k,1},a_{k,2}\}
is an orthonormal system of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
for
k_{3}>0
and
a_{k,j}=a_{-k,j}
if
k_{3}<0
, while the (complex) coefficients
\theta_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}
will be defined later.
It is well known that the family
\{a_{k,j}\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{2\pi\varepsilon}}e^{ik\cdot x}\},\ {{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,\varepsilon^{-1}}_{0},\ j\in\{1,2\}}}
is a complete orthogonal systems of
\mathbf{H}^{s}_{\varepsilon}
made by eigenfunctions of
-\Delta
. Moreover it is orthonormal in
\mathbf{L}^{2}_{\varepsilon}
.
Without losing of generality, we make a choice that will simplify some of the computations: when
k_{3}=0
we choose
a_{k,1}=(-k_{2}/\lvert k\rvert,k_{1}/\lvert k\rvert,0),\ a_{k,2}=(0,0,1)
if
k\in\Gamma_{+}^{\varepsilon}
,
a_{k,j}=a_{-k,j}
otherwise.
Now we define the Brownian motions. Our construction aims at obtaining a non trivial space covariance structure, while retaining the description in the Fourier basis, since this choice will simplify many computations thanks to
2.4
. The physical intuition behind our construction is discussed
2.11
. Begin with a correlation factor
\rho\in[-1,1]
. Then assume
\left(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}_{t},\mathbb{P}\right)
is a filtered probability space such that
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
is a complete probability space,
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
is a right continuous filtration and
\mathcal{F}_{0}
contains every
\mathbb{P}
null subset of
\Omega
.
Now, for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
\{W_{t}^{k,j}\},\ {{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0},\ j\in\{1,2\}}}
is a sequence of complex-valued Brownian motions adapted to
\mathcal{F}_{t}

such that

W^{-k,j}_{t}=(W^{k,j}_{t})^{*}
and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[W^{k,j}_{1},{W^{l,m}_{1}}^{*}\right]

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}2&\textit{if }k=l,\ m=j\\
2\rho&\textit{if }k=l,\ k_{3}=0,\ m\neq j;\end{cases}\quad\left[W^{k,j}_{\cdot},W^{l,m}_{\cdot}\right]_{t}=\begin{cases}2t&\textit{if }k=-l,\ m=j\\
2\rho t&\textit{if }k=-l,\ k_{3}=0,\ m\neq j.\end{cases}

Then we define

\displaystyle W^{\varepsilon}_{t}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0}\\
j\in\{1,2\}\end{subarray}}\sigma_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}W^{k,j}_{t},

with
\sigma_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}=\theta_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}a_{k,j}e^{ik\cdot x}
. The role of the factor
\rho
is to introduce some correlation between the vertical and horizontal components of the ‘2D’ modes (those who have
k_{3}=0
) of the random field.
With our choices, to ensure that
W^{\varepsilon}
takes values in a space of real valued functions, the coefficients must satisfy the condition

(2.6)

{\theta_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}}^{*}=\theta_{-k,j}^{\varepsilon}.

Remark 2.5
.

The complex Brownian motions
W^{k,j}_{t}
required can be constructed in the following way: let
\{B_{t}^{k,j}\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0}\\
j\in\{1,2\}\end{subarray}}
be a sequence of real, Brownian motions adapted to
\mathcal{F}_{t}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[B^{k,j}_{t}B^{l,m}_{s}\right]=\left(t\wedge s\right)\left(\delta_{j,m}\delta_{k,l}+\rho\delta_{\lvert j-m\rvert=1}\delta_{k,l}\delta_{k_{3},0}\right)

and then set

\displaystyle W^{k,j}_{t}=\begin{cases}B^{k,j}_{t}+iB^{-k,j}_{t}&\text{if }k\in\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{+}\\
B^{-k,j}_{t}-iB^{k,j}_{t}&\text{if }k\in\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{-}.\end{cases}

Finally, we make an explicit choice of the coefficients
\theta_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}
:

(2.7)

\displaystyle\theta_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\bm{1}_{\{N\leq\lvert k_{H}\rvert\leq 2N\}}\begin{cases}\frac{i}{C_{1,H}\lvert k\rvert}&\text{if }k_{3}=0,k\in\Gamma_{+}^{\varepsilon},\ j=1\\
\frac{-i}{C_{1,H}\lvert k\rvert}&\text{if }k_{3}=0,k\in\Gamma_{-}^{\varepsilon},\ j=1\\
\frac{1}{C_{2,H}\lvert k\rvert^{\gamma/2}}&\text{if }k_{3}=0,\ j=2\\
\frac{1}{C_{V}\lvert k\rvert^{\beta/2}}&\text{if }k_{3}\neq 0.\end{cases}

The parameters
\varepsilon,N,C_{1,H},C_{2,H},C_{V},\beta,\gamma
cannot be arbitrary. Indeed we will always assume in the following even if not specified:

Hypothesis 2.6
.

•

The width of the thin layer is
2\pi\varepsilon=\frac{2\pi}{N}
, i.e.
\varepsilon=\frac{1}{N}
for
N\in\mathbb{N}
.

•

\beta\geq 4,\ \gamma\geq 4.

•

C_{1,H}>\sqrt{\frac{\zeta_{H,0}}{\eta}},\ C_{2,H},\ C_{V}>0
.

More general choices for the phase and the sign of our coefficients are allowed, although the computations and the notation get heavier, while the decay rate (
\beta,\gamma\geq 4
) is optimal in our setting. For this reason we decide to avoid this level of generality.

Remark 2.7
.

Due to our choice of the coefficients
\theta_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}
and the vectors
a_{k,j}
it follows that for each
k
such that
k_{3}=0,\ N\leq\lvert k\rvert\leq 2N

\displaystyle\theta_{k,1}^{\varepsilon}a_{k,1}=\frac{i}{C_{1,H}}\frac{k^{\perp}}{\lvert k\rvert^{2}},\ \theta_{k,2}^{\varepsilon}a_{k,2}=\frac{1}{C_{2,H}}\frac{e_{3}}{\lvert k\rvert^{\gamma/2}}.

From which it follows that the 2D modes of our noise naturally split in horizontal and vertical components

\displaystyle\overline{W^{\varepsilon,H}}:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}_{0}^{3,N}\\
{k_{3}=0,j=1}\end{subarray}}\sigma_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}W_{k}^{j}\qquad\overline{W^{\varepsilon,3}}:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}_{0}^{3,N}\\
{k_{3}=0,j=2}\end{subarray}}\sigma_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}W_{k}^{j}

Moreover the components satisfy the relations

\displaystyle\overline{W^{\varepsilon,H}}(-x)=-\overline{W^{\varepsilon,H}}(x),\qquad\overline{W^{\varepsilon,3}}(-x)=\overline{W^{\varepsilon,3}}(x).

As shown in
[
6
]
, this anisotropy of the noise is the main responsible for the appearance of the alpha-term in this kind of models.

The space covariance function associated to our noise is

(2.8)

\displaystyle Q^{\varepsilon}(x,y)=\mathbb{E}\left[W^{\varepsilon}_{1}(x)\otimes{W^{\varepsilon}_{1}(y)}^{*}\right]=Q^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x,y)+\overline{Q^{\varepsilon}_{\rho}}(x,y)=\overline{Q^{\varepsilon}}(x,y)+\left(Q^{\varepsilon}\right)^{\prime}(x,y)+\overline{Q^{\varepsilon}_{\rho}}(x,y)

where we denoted by

\displaystyle\overline{Q^{\varepsilon}}(x,y)

\displaystyle=2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0}\\
k_{3}=0,\hskip 8.19447ptj\in\{1,2\}\end{subarray}}\left\lvert\theta_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}\right\rvert^{2}a_{k,j}\otimes a_{k,j}e^{ik\cdot(x-y)},\quad\left(Q^{\varepsilon}\right)^{\prime}(x,y)=2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0}\\
k_{3}\neq 0,\hskip 8.19447ptj\in\{1,2\}\end{subarray}}\left\lvert\theta_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}\right\rvert^{2}a_{k,j}\otimes a_{k,j}e^{ik\cdot(x-y)},

\displaystyle\overline{Q^{\varepsilon}_{\rho}}(x,y)

\displaystyle=2\rho\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0}\\
k_{3}=0\end{subarray}}\left(\theta_{k,1}^{\varepsilon}\left(\theta_{k,2}^{\varepsilon}\right)^{*}a_{k,1}\otimes a_{k,2}+\theta_{k,2}^{\varepsilon}\left(\theta_{k,1}^{\varepsilon}\right)^{*}a_{k,2}\otimes a_{k,1}\right)e^{ik\cdot(x-y)}.

Due to our choice, we have that
\overline{Q^{\varepsilon}}(x,y),

\left(Q^{\varepsilon}\right)^{\prime}(x,y)
and
\overline{Q^{\varepsilon}_{\rho}}(x,y)
are all translation invariant, therefore we simply write
Q^{\varepsilon}(x-y)
(resp.
Q^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x-y),\ \overline{Q^{\varepsilon}}(x-y),\ \left(Q^{\varepsilon}\right)^{\prime}(x-y),\ \overline{Q^{\varepsilon}_{\rho}}(x-y)
) in place of
Q^{\varepsilon}(x,y)
(resp.
Q^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x,y),\ \overline{Q^{\varepsilon}}(x,y),\ \left(Q^{\varepsilon}\right)^{\prime}(x,y),\ \overline{Q^{\varepsilon}_{\rho}}(x,y)
). In particular
Q^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x),\ \overline{Q^{\varepsilon}}(x),\ \left(Q^{\varepsilon}\right)^{\prime}(x)
are mirror symmetric, namely it holds

(2.9)

\displaystyle Q^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x)=Q^{\varepsilon}_{0}(-x),\ \overline{Q^{\varepsilon}}(x)=\overline{Q^{\varepsilon}}(-x),\ \left(Q^{\varepsilon}\right)^{\prime}(x)=\left(Q^{\varepsilon}\right)^{\prime}(-x).

On the contrary
\overline{Q^{\varepsilon}_{\rho}}(x)
is an odd function, in particular
\overline{Q^{\varepsilon}_{\rho}}(0)=0
. As a consequence, our noise satisfies the mirror symmetry property if and only if
\rho=0
.
Let us understand better the behavior of
Q^{\varepsilon}(0)
. Indeed, as discussed in
[
9
, Chapter 3]
, it plays a crucial in order to provide the dissipation properties of our limit object. Let us start considering
\left(Q^{\varepsilon}\right)^{\prime}(0)
, we have:

\displaystyle\left(Q^{\varepsilon}\right)^{\prime}(0)=2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0}\\
N\leq\lvert k_{H}\rvert\leq 2N\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{C_{V}^{2}\lvert k\rvert^{\beta}}\left(I-\frac{k\otimes k}{\lvert k\rvert^{2}}\right).

Arguing as in
[
11
, Section 2.3]
, it follows easily that
\left(Q^{\varepsilon}\right)^{\prime}(0)
is a diagonal matrix, moreover it holds

\displaystyle\left(Q^{\varepsilon}\right)^{\prime}(0)=2(e_{1}\otimes e_{1}+e_{2}\otimes e_{2})\eta^{\varepsilon}_{V,T}+2(e_{3}\otimes e_{3})\eta_{V,R}^{\varepsilon},

where we denoted by

\displaystyle\eta^{\varepsilon}_{V,T}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0},\ k_{3}\neq 0\\
N\leq\lvert k_{H}\rvert\leq 2N\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{C_{V}^{2}\lvert k\rvert^{\beta}}\left(1-\frac{\lvert k_{H}\rvert^{2}}{2\lvert k\rvert^{2}}\right),\quad\eta_{V,R}^{\varepsilon}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0},\ k_{3}\neq 0\\
N\leq\lvert k_{H}\rvert\leq 2N\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{C_{V}^{2}\lvert k\rvert^{\beta}}\left(1-\frac{\lvert k_{3}\rvert^{2}}{\lvert k\rvert^{2}}\right).

In particular both
\eta^{\varepsilon}_{V,T},\ \eta^{\varepsilon}_{V,R}
go to
0
as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
. Indeed it holds

\displaystyle\eta^{\varepsilon}_{V,T}+\eta^{\varepsilon}_{V,R}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0},\ k_{3}\neq 0\\
N\leq\lvert k_{H}\rvert\leq 2N\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\left(\lvert k_{H}\rvert^{2}+\lvert k_{3}\rvert^{2}\right)^{\beta/2}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{H}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{0}\\
N\leq\lvert k_{H}\rvert\leq 2N\end{subarray}}\sum_{k_{3}\in\mathbb{Z},\ k_{3}\neq 0}\frac{1}{\left(1+\lvert k_{3}\rvert^{2}\right)^{\beta/2}N^{\beta}}

(2.10)

\displaystyle\leq\zeta^{N}_{H,0}\zeta_{\beta}\frac{1}{N^{\beta-2}}=O(N^{2-\beta}).

In order to treat
\overline{Q^{\varepsilon}}(0)
we introduce a further splitting of the sum. We write

\displaystyle\overline{Q^{\varepsilon}}(x)=\overline{Q_{T}^{\varepsilon}}(x)+\overline{Q_{R}^{\varepsilon}}(x),

denoting by

\displaystyle\overline{Q^{\varepsilon}_{T}}(x)=2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0}\\
k_{3}=0,\hskip 8.19447ptj=1\end{subarray}}\left\lvert\theta_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}\right\rvert^{2}a_{k,j}\otimes a_{k,j}e^{ik\cdot x},\quad\overline{Q^{\varepsilon}_{R}}(x)=2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0}\\
k_{3}=0,\hskip 8.19447ptj=2\end{subarray}}\left\lvert\theta_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}\right\rvert^{2}a_{k,j}\otimes a_{k,j}e^{ik\cdot x}.

Of course,
\overline{Q^{\varepsilon}_{T}}(x),\overline{Q^{\varepsilon}_{R}}(x)
satisfy mirror symmetry property too. By definition it holds

\displaystyle\overline{Q^{\varepsilon}_{R}}(0)=2\left(e_{3}\otimes e_{3}\right)\eta^{\varepsilon}_{H,R},\quad\eta^{\varepsilon}_{H,R}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{H}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{0}\\
N\leq\lvert k_{H}\rvert\leq 2N\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{C_{2,H}^{2}\lvert k_{H}\rvert^{\gamma}}.

Therefore also
\eta^{\varepsilon}_{H,R}
goes to
0
as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
. Indeed, we have

(2.11)

\displaystyle\eta^{\varepsilon}_{H,R}=\frac{\zeta^{N}_{H,\gamma}N^{2-\gamma}}{C_{2,H}^{2}}=O(N^{2-\gamma}).

Lastly we need to study the asymptotic behavior of
\overline{Q^{\varepsilon}_{T}}(0)
which is the one we will keep track in the scaling limit. Arguing as in
[
11
, Section 2.3]
, it follows easily that also
\overline{Q^{\varepsilon}_{T}}(0)
is a diagonal matrix, moreover it holds

\displaystyle\overline{Q^{\varepsilon}_{T}}(0)=\frac{\zeta_{H,2}^{N}}{C_{1,H}^{2}}(e_{1}\otimes e_{1}+e_{2}\otimes e_{2})=\frac{\zeta_{H,2}}{C_{1,H}^{2}}(e_{1}\otimes e_{1}+e_{2}\otimes e_{2})+O(N^{-1}).

We set

\displaystyle\eta^{\varepsilon}_{T}=\eta^{\varepsilon}_{V,T}+\frac{\zeta^{N}_{H,2}}{C_{1,H}^{2}}=\frac{\zeta_{H,2}}{C_{1,H}^{2}}+O(N^{-1}),\quad\eta^{\varepsilon}_{R}=\eta^{\varepsilon}_{V,R}+\eta^{\varepsilon}_{H,R}=O(N^{-4}),\quad\eta_{T}=\frac{\zeta_{H,2}}{C_{1,H}^{2}}.

We state now some results on the covariance functions
Q^{\varepsilon}(x),

Q^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x),

\overline{Q_{\rho}^{\varepsilon}}(x),

\overline{Q^{\varepsilon}}(x),

\left(Q^{\varepsilon}\right)^{\prime}(x),

\overline{Q_{T}^{\varepsilon}}(x),

\overline{Q_{R}^{\varepsilon}}(x)
. The first ones are strongly related to the invariance by translation of the covariance functions, therefore holds for all of them. We give the detailed statement and proof only for
Q^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x)
, the others being analogous.

Lemma 2.8
.

For each
l,m\in\{1,2,3\}
it holds

(2.12)

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{l}Q^{\varepsilon}_{0}\right)(x-y)

\displaystyle=\partial_{x_{l}}Q^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x-y)=2i\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0}\\
j\in\{1,2\}\end{subarray}}\left\lvert\theta_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}\right\rvert^{2}k_{l}a_{k,j}\otimes a_{k,j}e^{ik\cdot(x-y)},

(2.13)

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{l}\partial_{m}Q^{\varepsilon}_{0}\right)(x-y)

\displaystyle=-\partial_{x_{l}}\partial_{y_{m}}Q^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x-y)=-2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0}\\
j\in\{1,2\}\end{subarray}}\left\lvert\theta_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}\right\rvert^{2}k_{l}k_{m}a_{k,j}\otimes a_{k,j}e^{ik\cdot(x-y)}.

In particular

(2.14)

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{l}Q^{\varepsilon}_{0}\right)(0)

\displaystyle=2i\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0}\\
j\in\{1,2\}\end{subarray}}\left\lvert\theta_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}\right\rvert^{2}k_{l}a_{k,j}\otimes a_{k,j},

(2.15)

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{l}\partial_{m}Q^{\varepsilon}_{0}\right)(0)

\displaystyle=-2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0}\\
j\in\{1,2\}\end{subarray}}\left\lvert\theta_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}\right\rvert^{2}k_{l}k_{m}a_{k,j}\otimes a_{k,j}.

Proof.

\left(\partial_{l}Q^{\varepsilon}_{0}\right)(x-y)=\partial_{x_{l}}Q^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x-y)
and
\left(\partial_{l}\partial_{m}Q^{\varepsilon}_{0}\right)(x-y)=-\partial_{x_{l}}\partial_{y_{m}}Q^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x-y)
by the chain rule. Then (
2.12
) and (
2.13
) follow from the definition of
Q^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x-y).

Setting
y=x
in the right hand side of (
2.12
) (resp. (
2.13
)) we get immediately

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{l}Q^{\varepsilon}_{0}\right)(0)

\displaystyle=2i\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0}\\
j\in\{1,2\}\end{subarray}}\left\lvert\theta_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}\right\rvert^{2}k_{l}a_{k,j}\otimes a_{k,j}

\displaystyle\text{(resp. }\left(\partial_{l}\partial_{m}Q^{\varepsilon}_{0}\right)(0)

\displaystyle=-2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0}\\
j\in\{1,2\}\end{subarray}}\left\lvert\theta_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}\right\rvert^{2}k_{l}k_{m}a_{k,j}\otimes a_{k,j}\text{)}.

∎

The other results we need on the covariance functions are strongly related to the mirror symmetry properties, therefore they hold true only for
Q^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x),\overline{Q^{\varepsilon}}(x),\ \left(Q^{\varepsilon}\right)^{\prime}(x),\ \overline{Q_{T}^{\varepsilon}}(x),\ \overline{Q_{R}^{\varepsilon}}(x)
. We give the detailed statement and proof only for
Q^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x)
, the others being analogous.

Lemma 2.9
.

It holds

(2.16)

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{l}Q^{\varepsilon}_{0}\right)(0)

\displaystyle=2i\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0}\\
j\in\{1,2\}\end{subarray}}\left\lvert\theta_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}\right\rvert^{2}k_{l}a_{k,j}\otimes a_{k,j}=0.

As a consequence, for each
l,m,n\in\{1,2,3\}
and
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3}_{\varepsilon}),\ g\in H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{3}_{\varepsilon})
it holds

(2.17)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0}\\
j\in\{1,2\}\end{subarray}}\langle\partial_{l}g\sigma_{k,j}^{l,\varepsilon},f\partial_{m}\sigma_{-k,j}^{n,\varepsilon}\rangle_{\varepsilon}=0.

Proof.

Since by the mirror symmetry property of the covariance function (
2.9
), the function
Q^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x)
is even and smooth, then
\left(\partial_{l}Q^{\varepsilon}_{0}\right)(0)=0
and (
2.16
) follows. In order to complete the proof, let us expand the left hand side of (
2.17
), obtaining by definition of the
\sigma_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}
above

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0}\\
j\in\{1,2\}\end{subarray}}\langle\partial_{l}g\sigma_{k,j}^{l,\varepsilon},f\partial_{m}\sigma_{-k,j}^{n,\varepsilon}\rangle_{\varepsilon}=\langle f,\partial_{l}g\rangle_{\varepsilon}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0}\\
j\in\{1,2\}\end{subarray}}\left\lvert\theta_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}\right\rvert^{2}k_{m}(a_{k,j})_{l}(a_{k,j})_{n}=0

due to (
2.16
).
∎

On the contrary, as discussed in
[
6
]
, the asymptotic behavior of
\nabla\overline{Q^{\varepsilon}_{\rho}}(0)
plays a crucial role in order to understand the alpha-term of our model. Therefore, the last result we provide in this section is a convergence property of the matrix

(2.18)

\displaystyle\{{\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,\gamma}}^{m,n}\}_{m,n\in\{1,2\}}=\partial_{n}\overline{Q^{\varepsilon,3,m}_{\rho}}(0)

and a uniform bound on
\nabla\overline{Q^{\varepsilon}_{\rho}}(0)
.

Lemma 2.10
.

It holds

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla\overline{Q^{\varepsilon}_{\rho}}(0)\right\|_{HS}\leq\frac{2\sqrt{2}\lvert\rho\rvert\zeta^{N}_{H,\gamma/2}N^{2-\gamma/2}}{C_{1,H}C_{2,H}}.

In particular

\displaystyle\{{\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,\gamma}}^{m,n}\}_{m,n\in\{1,2\}}=\partial_{n}\overline{Q^{\varepsilon,3,m}_{\rho}}(0)=\frac{\pi\rho\zeta_{H,\gamma/2}}{C_{1,H}C_{2,H}}\begin{bmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}N^{2-\gamma/2}+O(N^{1-\gamma/2}).

Proof.

Thanks to (
2.14
) and
2.7
it holds

(2.19)

\displaystyle\nabla\overline{Q^{\varepsilon}_{\rho}}(0)=\frac{2\rho}{C_{1,H}C_{2,H}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{H}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{0}\\
N\leq\lvert k_{H}\rvert\leq 2N\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\lvert k_{H}\rvert^{\gamma/2}}\left(e_{3}\otimes\frac{k_{H}^{\perp}}{\lvert k_{H}\rvert}\otimes\frac{k_{H}}{\lvert k_{H}\rvert}-\frac{k_{H}^{\perp}}{\lvert k_{H}\rvert}\otimes e_{3}\otimes\frac{k_{H}}{\lvert k_{H}\rvert}\right).

Therefore we have

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla\overline{Q^{\varepsilon}_{\rho}}(0)\right\|_{HS}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\lvert\rho\rvert}{C_{1,H}C_{2,H}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{H}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{0}\\
N\leq\lvert k_{H}\rvert\leq 2N\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\lvert k_{H}\rvert^{\gamma/2}}\left\|\left(e_{3}\otimes\frac{k_{H}^{\perp}}{\lvert k_{H}\rvert}\otimes\frac{k_{H}}{\lvert k_{H}\rvert}-\frac{k_{H}^{\perp}}{\lvert k_{H}\rvert}\otimes e_{3}\otimes\frac{k_{H}}{\lvert k_{H}\rvert}\right)\right\|_{HS}

(2.20)

\displaystyle=\frac{2\sqrt{2}\lvert\rho\rvert\zeta^{N}_{H,\gamma/2}N^{2-\gamma/2}}{C_{1,H}C_{2,H}}.

Due to (
2.19
), it holds

\displaystyle\partial_{\cdot}\overline{Q^{\varepsilon,3,\cdot}_{\rho}}(0)

\displaystyle=\frac{2\rho}{C_{1,H}C_{2,H}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{H}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{0}\\
N\leq\lvert k_{H}\rvert\leq 2N\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\lvert k_{H}\rvert^{\gamma/2}}\frac{k_{H}^{\perp}}{\lvert k_{H}\rvert}\otimes\frac{k_{H}}{\lvert k_{H}\rvert}

(2.21)

\displaystyle=\frac{2\rho}{C_{1,H}C_{2,H}N^{\gamma/2-2}}\left(\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{H}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{0}\\
1\leq\frac{\lvert k_{H}\rvert}{N}\leq 2\end{subarray}}\frac{N^{\gamma/2}}{\lvert k_{H}\rvert^{\gamma/2}}\frac{k_{H}^{\perp}}{\lvert k_{H}\rvert}\otimes\frac{k_{H}}{\lvert k_{H}\rvert}\right).

The function
f_{\gamma}(x)=\frac{1}{\lvert x\rvert^{\gamma/2+2}}(x^{\perp}\otimes x)
is smooth for
1\leq\lvert x\rvert\leq 2
, hence the Riemann sums satisfy

(2.22)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{H}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{0}\\
1\leq\frac{\lvert k_{H}\rvert}{N}\leq 2\end{subarray}}\frac{N^{\gamma/2}}{\lvert k_{H}\rvert^{\gamma/2}}\frac{k_{H}^{\perp}}{\lvert k_{H}\rvert}\otimes\frac{k_{H}}{\lvert k_{H}\rvert}=\int_{1\leq x\leq 2}f_{\gamma}(x)dx+O(N^{-1}).

Since we have

(2.23)

\displaystyle\int_{1\leq x\leq 2}f_{\gamma}(x)dx=\int_{1}^{2}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{1}{r^{\gamma/2-1}}\begin{bmatrix}-\sin(\theta)\cos(\theta)&-\sin^{2}(\theta)\\
\cos^{2}(\theta)&\sin(\theta)\cos(\theta)\end{bmatrix}drd\theta=\frac{\zeta_{H,\gamma/2}}{2}\begin{bmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{bmatrix},

combining (
2.3
), (
2.22
) and (
2.23
) the thesis follows.
∎

Remark 2.11
.

We highlight that our noise is an adaptation of the one constructed in
[
6
]
for a 2D-3C model, where a certain helicity of the noise is imposed to achieve an alpha-term in the limit. In that work the authors constructed a random field based on the notion of a ‘cylinder of vorticity’. A cylinder of vorticity is for them a special 3D vector field
V(x)
on
\mathbb{T}^{2}
that is decomposed as
V_{H}
, an horizontal component corresponding ideally to a point vortex, and
V_{3}e_{3}
a vertical component, with the property that
V_{H}
is an odd function of
x
, while
V_{3}
is even. By randomizing the position of this cylinder, they were able to use the covariance matrix of the field to define a Brownian motion. While we could not give the same description here, since we needed to express the noise in the Fourier basis to exploit crucial simplifications (cfr.
2.4
), our noise retains all the properties of the one used in that work. In particular, by splitting our noise as
W^{\varepsilon}(t,x)=\overline{W^{\varepsilon}}(t,x)+\left(W^{\varepsilon}(t,x)\right)^{\prime}
, where the first part comprises all the wavenumbers with
k_{3}=0
, and recalling the further orthogonal decomposition
\overline{W^{\varepsilon}}(t,x)=\overline{W^{\varepsilon,H}}(t,x)+\overline{W^{\varepsilon,3}}(t,x)e_{3}
, we see that this part of the noise correspond exactly to theirs. Indeed, in order to have a nonzero ‘off diagonal’ term
\overline{Q^{\varepsilon}_{\rho}}(0)
in the covariance, we had to introduce a correlation between the horizontal and the vertical component, which was inherent in the structure of the cylinder, and breaks mirror symmetry for the horizontal component, which we did with the special choice of the coefficients, that is, we made it so that
\overline{W^{\varepsilon,H}}
is an odd function of
x
, while
\overline{W^{\varepsilon,3}}
is even, exactly like in the cylinder case.
Finally, it is easy to check that our main assumption on the decay of the noise coefficients (
\beta,\gamma\geq 4
, cfr.
2.6
) is equivalent to the main assumption of that work, that is, a uniform control on the second derivatives of the covariance matrix, see
[
6
, Hypothesis 3.3]
. In
Appendix B
we compute the mean helicity of our noise and stress its relation with the strength of the alpha-term in the limit PDE.

2.4.
Main Results

Following the ideas introduced in
section 1
, we fix
T\in(0,+\infty)
and we are interested in studying the properties of the following stochastic model on
\mathbb{T}^{3}_{\varepsilon}
:

(2.24)

\begin{cases}dB^{\varepsilon}_{t}&=\eta\Delta B^{\varepsilon}_{t}dt+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0}\\
j\in\{1,2\}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}}B^{\varepsilon}_{t}\circ dW^{k,j}_{t},\\
\operatorname{div}B^{\varepsilon}_{t}&=0,\\
B^{\varepsilon}_{t}|_{t=0}&=B^{\varepsilon}_{0},\end{cases}

with the coefficients
\sigma_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}
defined in
subsection 2.3
. We start rewriting it in Itô form. First, let us observe that for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0},\ j\in\{1,2\}
we have due to the linearity of
\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}}B^{\varepsilon}_{t}\circ dW^{k,j}_{t}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}}B^{\varepsilon}_{t}dW^{k,j}_{t}+\frac{1}{2}\left[\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}}B^{\varepsilon}_{\cdot},W^{k,j}_{\cdot}\right]_{t}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}}B^{\varepsilon}_{t}dW^{k,j}_{t}+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}}\left[B^{\varepsilon}_{\cdot},W^{k,j}_{\cdot}\right]_{t}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}}B^{\varepsilon}_{t}dW^{k,j}_{t}+\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{-k,j}^{\varepsilon}}B^{\varepsilon}_{t}dt+\rho\bm{1}_{\{k_{3}=0,\ l\neq j\}}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{-k,l}^{\varepsilon}}B^{\varepsilon}_{t}dt.

Therefore the equation for
B^{\varepsilon}_{t}
can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\begin{cases}dB^{\varepsilon}_{t}&=\left(\eta\Delta+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0}\\
j\in\{1,2\}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{-k,j}^{\varepsilon}}+\rho\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0}\\
k_{3}=0\end{subarray}}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{k,1}^{\varepsilon}}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{-k,2}^{\varepsilon}}+\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{k,2}^{\varepsilon}}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{-k,1}^{\varepsilon}}\right)B^{\varepsilon}_{t}dt\\
&+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0}\\
j\in\{1,2\}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}}B^{\varepsilon}_{t}dW^{k,j}_{t},\\
\operatorname{div}B^{\varepsilon}_{t}&=0,\\
B^{\varepsilon}_{t}|_{t=0}&=B^{\varepsilon}_{0}.\end{cases}

We are left to study
\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0}\\
j\in\{1,2\}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{-k,j}^{\varepsilon}}+\rho\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0}\\
k_{3}=0\end{subarray}}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{k,1}^{\varepsilon}}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{-k,2}^{\varepsilon}}+\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{k,2}^{\varepsilon}}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{-k,1}^{\varepsilon}}
. Its analysis exploits strongly the properties of the covariance function of our noise, i.e. translation invariance and (almost) mirror symmetry and it is contained in the following Lemma. Even if its proof is quite standard, see
[
9
, Section 3.4.1]
we prefer to include it in
Appendix A
for the convenience of the reader.

Lemma 2.12
.

If
F:\mathbb{T}^{3}_{\varepsilon}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
is a smooth, zero mean divergence free vector field, then it holds

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0}\\
j\in\{1,2\}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{-k,j}^{\varepsilon}}F+\rho\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0}\\
k_{3}=0\end{subarray}}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{k,1}^{\varepsilon}}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{-k,2}^{\varepsilon}}+\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{k,2}^{\varepsilon}}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{-k,1}^{\varepsilon}}\right)F

\displaystyle=\Lambda^{\varepsilon}F+\Lambda_{\rho}^{\varepsilon}F,\quad

where in the formula above we denoted by
\Lambda^{\varepsilon}
the differential operator

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\varepsilon}F=\eta^{\varepsilon}_{T}(\partial^{2}_{11}+\partial^{2}_{22})F+\eta^{\varepsilon}_{R}\partial^{2}_{33}F

and by
\Lambda_{\rho}^{\varepsilon}
the differential operator

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\rho}^{\varepsilon}F=-\sum_{l\in\{1,2\}}\partial_{l}\overline{Q^{\varepsilon}_{\rho}}(0)\cdot\nabla F_{l}=-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\{1,2,3\}\\
l\in\{1,2\}\end{subarray}}\partial_{l}\overline{Q^{\varepsilon,\cdot,j}_{\rho}}(0)\partial_{j}F_{l}.

Thanks to
2.12
, equation (
2.24
) can be rewritten in Itô form as:

(2.25)

\begin{cases}dB^{\varepsilon}_{t}&=\left(\eta\Delta+\Lambda^{\varepsilon}+\Lambda^{\varepsilon}_{\rho}\right)B^{\varepsilon}_{t}dt+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0}\\
j\in\{1,2\}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}}B^{\varepsilon}_{t}dW^{k,j}_{t},\\
\operatorname{div}B^{\varepsilon}_{t}&=0,\\
B^{\varepsilon}_{t}|_{t=0}&=B^{\varepsilon}_{0},\end{cases}

We give now the definition of weak solution for the Stochastic equation of the magnetic field (
2.25
).

Definition 2.13
.

A stochastic process

\displaystyle B^{\varepsilon}\in C_{\mathcal{F}}([0,T];\mathbf{L}^{2}_{\varepsilon})\cap L^{2}_{\mathcal{F}}([0,T];\mathbf{H}^{1}_{\varepsilon})

is a weak solution of equation (
2.25
) if, for every
\phi\in\mathbf{H}^{2}_{\varepsilon}
, we have

(2.26)

\displaystyle\langle B^{\varepsilon}_{t},\phi\rangle_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\langle B^{\varepsilon}_{0},\phi\rangle_{\varepsilon}+\eta\int_{0}^{t}\langle B^{\varepsilon}_{s},\Delta\phi\rangle_{\varepsilon}\,ds+\int_{0}^{t}\langle B^{\varepsilon}_{s},\Lambda^{\varepsilon}\phi\rangle_{\varepsilon}\,ds+\int_{0}^{t}\langle\Lambda^{\varepsilon}_{\rho}B^{\varepsilon}_{s},\phi\rangle_{\varepsilon}\,ds+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0}\\
j\in\{1,2\}\end{subarray}}\int_{0}^{t}\langle\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}}B^{\varepsilon}_{s},\phi\rangle_{\varepsilon}dW^{k,j}_{s}.

for every
t\in[0,T],\ \mathbb{P}-a.s.

Due to the fact that equation (
2.25
) is linear, the existence and uniqueness of solutions in the sense of
2.13
is a standard fact which follows by the abstract theory of
[
2
, Chapters 3-5]
, see also
[
8
, Section 3.1]
. Indeed the following holds.

Theorem 2.14
.

For each
B^{\varepsilon}_{0}\in\mathbf{L}^{2}_{\varepsilon}
there exists a unique weak solution of system (
2.25
) in the sense of
2.13
.

We are now ready to introduce the mean and the fluctuation in the third component of the process
B^{\varepsilon}
which are the main objects of our analysis. Therefore, let

\displaystyle\overline{B^{\varepsilon}_{t}}:=M_{\varepsilon}B^{\varepsilon}_{t},\ {{B}^{\varepsilon}_{t}}^{\prime}:=N_{\varepsilon}B^{\varepsilon}_{t}.

More in general, in order to simplify the notation, in the following we will denote the projection operator
M_{\varepsilon}
(resp.
N_{\varepsilon}
) by
\overline{\cdot}
(resp.
\cdot^{\prime}
) forgetting the dependence from
\varepsilon
.
Thanks to
Theorem 2.14
and the fact that
M_{\varepsilon}
and
N_{\varepsilon}
are projection operator and commute with the operator
\operatorname{div}
, see
2.3
, we have in particular that

\displaystyle\overline{B^{\varepsilon}}\in L^{2}_{\mathcal{F}}(0,T;\mathbf{H}^{1}_{\varepsilon})\cap C_{\mathcal{F}}(0,T;\mathbf{L}^{2}_{\varepsilon}),\quad{{B}^{\varepsilon}}^{\prime}\in L^{2}_{\mathcal{F}}(0,T;\mathbf{H}^{1}_{\varepsilon})\cap C_{\mathcal{F}}(0,T;\mathbf{L}^{2}_{\varepsilon}).

For each
\phi\in\mathbf{H}^{2}_{\varepsilon}
, considering
\overline{\phi}
and
\phi^{\prime}
as test function in (
2.26
) we obtain that
\overline{B^{\varepsilon}_{t}}
and
{{B}^{\varepsilon}_{t}}^{\prime}
satisfies

\displaystyle\langle\overline{B^{\varepsilon}_{t}},\phi\rangle_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\langle\overline{B^{\varepsilon}_{0}},\phi\rangle_{\varepsilon}+\eta\int_{0}^{t}\langle\overline{B^{\varepsilon}_{s}},\Delta\phi\rangle_{\varepsilon}\,ds+\int_{0}^{t}\langle\overline{B^{\varepsilon}_{s}},\Lambda^{\varepsilon}\phi\rangle_{\varepsilon}\,ds+\int_{0}^{t}\langle\Lambda^{\varepsilon}_{\rho}\overline{B^{\varepsilon}_{s}},\phi\rangle_{\varepsilon}\,ds

(2.27)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0}\\
k_{3}=0,\hskip 8.19447ptj\in\{1,2\}\end{subarray}}\int_{0}^{t}\langle\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}}\overline{B^{\varepsilon}_{s}},\phi\rangle_{\varepsilon}dW^{k,j}_{s}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0}\\
k_{3}\neq 0,\hskip 8.19447ptj\in\{1,2\}\end{subarray}}\int_{0}^{t}\langle\overline{\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}}{{B}^{\varepsilon}_{s}}^{\prime}},\phi\rangle_{\varepsilon}dW^{k,j}_{s},

\displaystyle\langle{{B}^{\varepsilon}_{t}}^{\prime},{\phi}\rangle_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\langle{{B}^{\varepsilon}_{0}}^{\prime},{\phi}\rangle_{\varepsilon}+\eta\int_{0}^{t}\langle{{B}^{\varepsilon}_{s}}^{\prime},\Delta{\phi}\rangle_{\varepsilon}\,ds+\int_{0}^{t}\langle{{B}^{\varepsilon}_{s}}^{\prime},\Lambda^{\varepsilon}{\phi}\rangle_{\varepsilon}\,ds+\int_{0}^{t}\langle\Lambda^{\varepsilon}_{\rho}{{B}^{\varepsilon}_{s}}^{\prime},{\phi}\rangle_{\varepsilon}\,ds

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0}\\
k_{3}=0,\hskip 8.19447ptj\in\{1,2\}\end{subarray}}\int_{0}^{t}\langle\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}}{{B}^{\varepsilon}_{s}}^{\prime},{\phi}\rangle_{\varepsilon}dW^{k,j}_{s}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0}\\
k_{3}\neq 0,\hskip 8.19447ptj\in\{1,2\}\end{subarray}}\int_{0}^{t}\langle\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}}\overline{B^{\varepsilon}_{s}},{\phi}\rangle_{\varepsilon}dW^{k,j}_{s}

(2.28)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0}\\
k_{3}\neq 0,\hskip 8.19447ptj\in\{1,2\}\end{subarray}}\int_{0}^{t}\langle\left(\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}}{{B}^{\varepsilon}_{s}}^{\prime}\right)^{\prime},{\phi}\rangle_{\varepsilon}dW^{k,j}_{s}

for every
t\in[0,T],\ \mathbb{P}-a.s.
Namely
\overline{B^{\varepsilon}_{t}}
and
{{B}^{\varepsilon}_{t}}^{\prime}
are weak solutions of the following SPDEs:

(2.29)

\begin{cases}d\overline{B^{\varepsilon}_{t}}&=\left(\eta\Delta+\Lambda^{\varepsilon}+\Lambda^{\varepsilon}_{\rho}\right)\overline{B^{\varepsilon}_{t}}dt+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0}\\
k_{3}=0,\hskip 8.19447ptj\in\{1,2\}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}}\overline{B^{\varepsilon}_{t}}dW^{k,j}_{t}\\
&+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0}\\
k_{3}\neq 0,\hskip 8.19447ptj\in\{1,2\}\end{subarray}}\overline{\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}}{{B}^{\varepsilon}_{t}}^{\prime}}dW^{k,j}_{t},\\
\operatorname{div}\overline{B^{\varepsilon}_{t}}&=0,\\
\overline{B^{\varepsilon}_{t}}|_{t=0}&=\overline{B^{\varepsilon}_{0}},\end{cases}

(2.30)

\begin{cases}d{{B}^{\varepsilon}_{t}}^{\prime}&=\left(\eta\Delta+\Lambda^{\varepsilon}+\Lambda^{\varepsilon}_{\rho}\right){{B}^{\varepsilon}_{t}}^{\prime}dt+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0}\\
k_{3}=0,\hskip 8.19447ptj\in\{1,2\}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}}{{B}^{\varepsilon}_{t}}^{\prime}dW^{k,j}_{t}\\
&+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0}\\
k_{3}\neq 0,\hskip 8.19447ptj\in\{1,2\}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}}\overline{B^{\varepsilon}_{t}}dW^{k,j}_{t}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0}\\
k_{3}\neq 0,\hskip 8.19447ptj\in\{1,2\}\end{subarray}}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}}{{B}^{\varepsilon}_{t}}^{\prime}\right)^{\prime}dW^{k,j}_{t},\\
\operatorname{div}{{B}^{\varepsilon}_{t}}^{\prime}&=0,\\
{{B}^{\varepsilon}_{t}}^{\prime}|_{t=0}&={{B}^{\varepsilon}_{0}}^{\prime}.\end{cases}

As discussed in
section 1
, we study the convergence of
\overline{B^{\varepsilon}_{t}}
in the simultaneous scaling limit of both separation of scales, i.e. considering a noise which concentrates on smaller and smaller scales as described in
subsection 2.3
, and of the width of our thin layer, namely considering
\varepsilon=\frac{1}{N}\rightarrow 0
as discussed in
[
22
,
21
,
23
]
. In order to do so, we need to introduce some few objects: we denote by

\displaystyle\overline{A^{\varepsilon}_{t}}=K_{\varepsilon}[\overline{B^{\varepsilon}_{t}}],\quad{{A}^{\varepsilon}_{t}}^{\prime}=K_{\varepsilon}[{{B}^{\varepsilon}_{t}}^{\prime}].

Thanks to the regularity properties of the Biot-Savart operator, see
subsubsection 2.2.1
, we have that

\displaystyle\overline{A^{\varepsilon}}\in L^{2}_{\mathcal{F}}(0,T;\mathbf{H}^{2}_{\varepsilon})\cap C_{\mathcal{F}}(0,T;\mathbf{H}^{1}_{\varepsilon}),\quad{{A}^{\varepsilon}}^{\prime}\in L^{2}_{\mathcal{F}}(0,T;\mathbf{H}^{2}_{\varepsilon})\cap C_{\mathcal{F}}(0,T;\mathbf{H}^{1}_{\varepsilon}),

moreover

\displaystyle\overline{A^{\varepsilon}}=(\overline{A^{H,\varepsilon}},\overline{A^{3,\varepsilon}})^{t}=(-\nabla_{H}^{\perp}(-\Delta)^{-1}\overline{B^{3,\varepsilon}},(-\Delta)^{-1}\nabla^{\perp}_{H}\cdot\overline{B^{H,\varepsilon}})^{t}.

Lastly we introduce our limit objects. Following the idea first introduced in
[
11
]
, we expect that our limit objects satisfy a PDE with an additional second order operator with intensity related to

\displaystyle\operatorname{lim}_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0}\\
j\in\{1,2\}\end{subarray}}\left\|\sigma_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}\right\|_{\varepsilon}^{2}.

Besides to the fact that the limit above is
0
, in the simultaneous separation of scale, thin layer limit, according to the discussion in
subsection 2.3
, the final deterministic objects keep memory of the operators
\Lambda^{\varepsilon},\ \Lambda^{\varepsilon}_{\rho}
defined above. Indeed, denoting by
\overline{A^{3}},\ \overline{B^{3}}
the unique weak solutions of the following linear 2D PDEs

(2.31)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\overline{A^{3,\gamma}_{t}}&=(\eta+\eta_{T})\Delta\overline{A^{3,\gamma}_{t}}\quad x\in\mathbb{T}^{2},\ t\in(0,T)\\
\overline{A^{3,\gamma}_{t}}|_{t=0}&=\overline{A^{3}_{0}},\end{cases}

(2.32)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\overline{B^{3,\gamma}_{t}}&=(\eta+\eta_{T})\Delta\overline{B^{3,\gamma}_{t}}+\operatorname{div}\left(\mathcal{R}_{\gamma}\nabla^{\perp}\overline{A^{3,\gamma}_{t}}\right)\quad x\in\mathbb{T}^{2},\ t\in(0,T)\\
\overline{B^{3,\gamma}_{t}}|_{t=0}&=\overline{B^{3}_{0}},\end{cases}

where
\eta_{T}=\zeta_{H,2}
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\gamma}=\begin{cases}\frac{2\pi\rho\log{2}}{C_{1,H}C_{2,H}}\begin{bmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}&\text{if }\gamma=4\\
0&\text{if }\gamma>4,\end{cases}

our main result reads as follows:

Theorem 2.15
.

Assuming
2.6
and

(2.33)

\displaystyle\overline{B^{3,\varepsilon}_{0}}\rightharpoonup\overline{B^{3}_{0}}\text{ in }\dot{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}),\quad\overline{A^{3,\varepsilon}_{0}}\rightharpoonup\overline{A^{3}_{0}}\text{ in }\dot{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}),\quad\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1)}\frac{\left\|{{B}^{3,\varepsilon}_{0}}^{\prime}\right\|_{\varepsilon}^{2}}{\varepsilon},\quad\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1)}\frac{\left\|{{A}^{\varepsilon}_{0}}^{\prime}\right\|_{\varepsilon}^{2}}{\varepsilon}<+\infty,

there exists
N_{0}
large enough such that for each
N\geq N_{0}
, for each
\theta_{1}\in(0,1],\ \theta_{2}\in(0,\theta_{1}),\ \delta\in(0,\theta_{2})
we have

(2.34)

\displaystyle\operatorname{sup}_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\overline{B^{3,\varepsilon}_{t}}-\overline{B^{3,\gamma}_{t}}\right\|_{\dot{H}^{-\theta_{1}}}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim\begin{cases}\left\|\overline{B^{3}_{0}}-\overline{B^{3,\varepsilon}_{0}}\right\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{-\theta_{1}}}+\left\|\overline{A^{3}_{0}}-\overline{A^{3,\varepsilon}_{0}}\right\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{-\theta_{2}}}+\frac{1}{N^{\frac{(\theta_{2}-\delta)(\theta_{1}-\theta_{2})}{(1+\delta)}}}\\
\text{if }\gamma=4,\\
\left\|\overline{B^{3}_{0}}-\overline{B^{3,\varepsilon}_{0}}\right\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{-\theta_{1}}}+\left\|\overline{A^{3}_{0}}-\overline{A^{3,\varepsilon}_{0}}\right\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{-\theta_{2}}}+\frac{1}{N^{\gamma-4}}+\frac{1}{N^{\frac{(\theta_{2}-\delta)(\theta_{1}-\theta_{2})}{(1+\delta)}}}\\
\text{if }\gamma>4;\end{cases}

(2.35)

\displaystyle\operatorname{sup}_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\overline{A^{3,\varepsilon}_{t}}-\overline{A^{3,\gamma}_{t}}\right\|_{\dot{H}^{-\theta_{2}}}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\overline{A^{3,\varepsilon}_{0}}-\overline{A^{3}_{0}}\right\|_{\dot{H}^{-\theta_{2}}}^{2}+\frac{1}{N^{\theta_{2}}}+\frac{1}{N^{\frac{2(\theta_{2}-\delta)}{1+\delta}}}.

The hidden constants in (
2.34
), (
2.35
) depend on
\eta,

\beta,

\gamma,

C_{V},

C_{1,H},

C_{2,H},

\lvert\rho\rvert
,
T
,
\operatorname{sup}_{\varepsilon\in(0,1)}{\left\|\overline{B^{3,\varepsilon}_{0}}\right\|^{2}},

\operatorname{sup}_{\varepsilon\in(0,1)}{\left\|\overline{A^{3,\varepsilon}_{0}}\right\|^{2}}
,
\operatorname{sup}_{\varepsilon\in(0,1)}\frac{{\left\|{{B}^{3,\varepsilon}_{0}}^{\prime}\right\|^{2}_{\varepsilon}}}{{\varepsilon}},

\operatorname{sup}_{\varepsilon\in(0,1)}\frac{{\left\|{{A}^{3,\varepsilon}_{0}}^{\prime}\right\|^{2}_{\varepsilon}}}{{\varepsilon}},

\theta_{1},

\theta_{2}
,
\delta
.
In particular

\displaystyle\operatorname{sup}_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\overline{B^{3,\varepsilon}_{t}}-\overline{B^{3,\gamma}_{t}}\right\|_{\dot{H}^{-\theta_{1}}}^{2}\right]+\operatorname{sup}_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\overline{A^{3,\varepsilon}_{t}}-\overline{A^{3,\gamma}_{t}}\right\|_{\dot{H}^{-\theta_{2}}}^{2}\right]\rightarrow 0\quad\text{as }N\rightarrow+\infty.

Remark 2.16
.

The explicit dependence of the hidden constants in (
2.34
), (
2.35
) in terms of
\theta_{1},\ \theta_{2}
and
\delta
can be found in
section 4
, see
4.5
and
4.6
.

Remark 2.17
.

Our assumption (
2.33
) is for example satisfied if
B^{\varepsilon}_{0}
can be decomposed as

\displaystyle B^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})=\overline{B}_{0}(x_{1},x_{2})+{B}^{\prime}_{0}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\frac{x_{3}}{\varepsilon}\right),

where, calling
{B^{\varepsilon}_{0}}^{\prime}=B_{0}^{\prime}\left(\cdot,\cdot,\frac{\cdot}{\varepsilon}\right)
, we have

\displaystyle\overline{B}_{0}\in\dot{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{3}),\quad{B}^{\prime}_{0}\in\dot{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3}),\quad\int_{\mathbb{T}}B^{\prime}_{0}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})dx_{3}=0\ a.e.\ (x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{T}^{2}

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}\overline{B}_{0}=0,\quad\operatorname{div}{B^{\varepsilon}_{0}}^{\prime}=0\quad\forall N\in\mathbb{N}.

Remark 2.18
.

Due to
2.6
,
\eta_{T}
cannot be arbitrarily large in our model. Indeed the following holds

\displaystyle\eta_{T}=\frac{\zeta_{H,2}}{C_{1,H}^{2}}<\frac{\zeta_{H,2}}{\zeta_{H,0}}\eta\approx 0.462\eta.

Remark 2.19
.

Contrary to other results related to ours, see
[
11
,
4
,
6
,
7
,
23
]
, we do not argue by compactness but we provide a quantitative result in the original probability space. Besides from the fact that this makes the result stronger, we are forced to argue in this way. Indeed, we can obtain uniform estimates in a common topology only for
\overline{B^{3,\varepsilon}_{\cdot}},\ \overline{A^{3,\varepsilon}_{\cdot}}
exploiting the fact that they are independent from the third variable and we can see them as functions from
\mathbb{T}^{2}
to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. This seems not possible for quantities related to
{{B}^{\varepsilon}_{\cdot}}^{\prime}
. Due to the lack of compactness for quantities related to
{{B}^{\varepsilon}_{\cdot}}^{\prime}
, we cannot find by Skorokhod’s representation theorem an auxiliary probability space where either (
2.4
), (
2.4
) hold and
(\overline{B^{3,\varepsilon}_{\cdot}},\ \overline{A^{3,\varepsilon}_{\cdot}})
converge a.s. to some limit objects. Therefore we cannot pass to the limit in equation (
2.4
) showing that the limit objects satisfy (
2.31
), (
2.32
).

2.5.
Strategy of the Proof

The proof of
Theorem 2.15
relies on rewriting in mild form
\overline{B^{3,\varepsilon}_{t}},\ \overline{A^{3,\varepsilon}_{t}},\ \overline{M^{3}_{t}},\ \overline{A^{3}_{t}}
, see
4.1
, showing, in a quantitative way, that the stochastic convolutions approach
0
when considering negative Sobolev norms in
\mathbb{T}^{2}
and having a good control on the pieces associated to the alpha-term in (
2.29
), (
2.32
). This is the object of
4.4
,
4.5
and
4.6
. These results (and actually also stronger ones) would be easily true if the following holds:
If
\left\|B_{0}^{\varepsilon}\right\|_{\varepsilon}^{2}=O(\varepsilon)
, then also

(2.36)

\displaystyle\left\|{B^{\varepsilon}}\right\|_{C_{\mathcal{F}}(0,T;\mathbf{L}^{2}_{\varepsilon})}^{2}+\left\|{B^{\varepsilon}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\mathcal{F}}(0,T;\mathbf{H}^{1}_{\varepsilon})}^{2}=O(\varepsilon).

Unfortunately, due to the stochastic stretching terms, equation (
2.36
) seems completely out of reach if one is interested on having a nontrivial operator in the limit
\operatorname{lim}_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\Lambda^{\varepsilon}
, see
[
5
, Appendix 2]
. Even if the operator
\Lambda^{\varepsilon}_{\rho}
and the correlation between the components of the noise
W^{\varepsilon}_{t}
makes system (
2.25
) more complicated, they do not increase the difficulties in order to obtain (
2.36
). We rely on the following idea, which we inherited by
[
22
,
23
]
: since the equation for
\overline{B^{\varepsilon}_{t}}
is a perturbation of a 2D-3C system, we can work, as in
[
6
]
, considering differently the horizontal and the vertical component of the magnetic field
\overline{B^{\varepsilon}_{t}}
. Since the third component
\overline{B^{3,\varepsilon}_{t}}
corresponds, in a vague sense, to the unique non null component associated to the
\operatorname{curl}
of a 2D divergence free vector field, according to
[
4
]
,
[
3
]
,
[
7
]
, we expect that, if we were able to control the perturbation with respect to the 2D-3C model, it behaves well in term of (
2.36
), see
[
6
]
. This is not true when considering
\overline{B^{H,\varepsilon}_{t}}
. Indeed, having a good control on the stochastic stretching terms appearing in the equation of
\overline{B^{H,\varepsilon}_{t}}
is as difficult as proving (
2.36
) and keeping a nontrivial operator in the limit due to the fact that, without exploiting some geometric features of the model, the term

(2.37)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0}\\
k_{3}=0,\hskip 8.19447ptj\in\{1,2\}\end{subarray}}\int_{0}^{T}\left\|\overline{B^{\varepsilon}_{s}}\cdot\nabla\sigma_{k,j}^{H,\varepsilon}\right\|_{\varepsilon}^{2}ds,

appearing in the energy estimates, seems to blow-up. The idea is, therefore, of considering the potential field
\overline{A^{\varepsilon}_{t}}
associated to
\overline{B^{\varepsilon}_{t}}
. Thanks to (
2.1
),
\overline{B^{H,\varepsilon}_{t}}=-\nabla^{\perp}_{H}\overline{A^{3,\varepsilon}_{t}}
and, formally, due to
3.2
below, it solves

\displaystyle d\overline{A^{3,\varepsilon}_{t}}

\displaystyle=\left(\eta\Delta+\Lambda^{\varepsilon}\right)\overline{A^{3,\varepsilon}_{t}}dt+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0}\\
k_{3}=0,\hskip 8.19447ptj\in\{1,2\}\end{subarray}}\sigma_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\overline{A^{3,\varepsilon}_{t}}dW^{k,j}_{t}

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0}\\
k_{3}\neq 0,\hskip 8.19447ptj\in\{1,2\}\end{subarray}}Q\left[\overline{\sigma_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla{{A}^{3,\varepsilon}_{t}}^{\prime}+\partial_{3}\sigma_{k,j}^{\varepsilon}\cdot{{A}^{3,\varepsilon}_{t}}^{\prime}}\right]dW^{k,j}_{t}.

Notice that, due to
A.1
,
\Lambda^{\varepsilon}_{\rho}\overline{B^{\varepsilon}_{t}}
is a vector having the first two components null, therefore it does not affect the equation for
\overline{B^{H,\varepsilon}_{t}}
.
Now, the bad stretching term (
2.37
) has disappeared at this level of regularity, and to show that
\overline{A^{3,\varepsilon}_{t}}
behaves well in term of (
2.36
) we are left to analyze this perturbation of the 2D-3C model. In order to show that the equations for
\overline{A^{3,\varepsilon}_{t}},\ \overline{B^{3,\varepsilon}_{t}}

are just a regular perturbation of the 2D-3C model we need either some smallness assumptions on the coefficient
\sigma_{k,j}^{\varepsilon},\ k_{3}\neq 0
in term of the derivative of the covariance function associated to the noise, see
subsection 2.3
, and some bounds on
{{B}^{\varepsilon}_{t}}^{\prime}
. Also in this case, the analysis of
{{B}^{3,\varepsilon}_{t}}^{\prime}
is relatively easy thanks to our assumptions on the noise which allows to control the stochastic stretching terms, see
subsection 2.3
. Lastly we need a good control on
{{B}^{H,\varepsilon}_{t}}^{\prime}
. This term seems again critical for what concerns the stochastic stretching, indeed a term of the form

(2.38)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0}\\
k_{3}=0,\hskip 8.19447ptj\in\{1,2\}\end{subarray}}\int_{0}^{T}\left\|{{B}^{\varepsilon}_{s}}^{\prime}\cdot\nabla\sigma_{k,j}^{H,\varepsilon}\right\|_{\varepsilon}^{2}ds

appears when computing energy estimates. Besides its form analogous to (
2.37
), the term (
2.38
) needs a different treatment than the others. For this term, we are able to exploit the special geometry of the thin layer, namely the Poincaré inequality (
2.5
) (with constant
\varepsilon
) holds when considering
{{B}^{\varepsilon}_{t}}^{\prime}
, counterbalancing the gradients of the coefficients. Indeed, applying Hölder inequality to relation (
2.38
), the explosive behavior of
\left\|\nabla\sigma_{k,j}^{H,\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{3}_{\varepsilon})}
is absorbed thanks to (
2.5
) and we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3,N}_{0}\\
k_{3}=0,\hskip 8.19447ptj\in\{1,2\}\end{subarray}}\int_{0}^{T}\left\|{{B}^{\varepsilon}_{s}}^{\prime}\cdot\nabla\sigma_{k,j}^{H,\varepsilon}\right\|_{\varepsilon}^{2}ds

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{\zeta_{H,0}}{C_{1,H}^{2}}+o(1)\right)\int_{0}^{T}\left\|\nabla{{B}^{\varepsilon}_{s}}^{\prime}\right\|^{2}_{\varepsilon}ds.

We refer to the analysis of
I^{2}_{t}
in
3.3
below for details.
Since we already have good estimates on
\int_{0}^{T}\left\|\nabla{{B}^{3,\varepsilon}_{s}}^{\prime}\right\|^{2}_{\varepsilon}ds
and
2.6
holds, (
2.38
) can be absorbed thanks to the dissipation properties of (
2.30
) obtaining a good control on the stochastic stretching term. Unfortunately this is not enough: energy estimates for
{{B}^{H,\varepsilon}_{t}}^{\prime}
involves also terms related to
\int_{0}^{T}\left\|\nabla\overline{B^{H,\varepsilon}_{t}}\right\|_{\varepsilon}^{2}ds
which are out of reach as discussed before. For this reason, we replace the analysis of
{{B}^{H,\varepsilon}_{t}}^{\prime}
with a term which involves at most
\int_{0}^{T}\left\|\nabla\overline{B^{3,\varepsilon}_{t}}\right\|_{\varepsilon}^{2}ds
and
\int_{0}^{T}\left\|\nabla\overline{A^{3,\varepsilon}_{t}}\right\|_{\varepsilon}^{2}ds
when computing its energy estimates and combined with
{{B}^{3,\varepsilon}_{t}}^{\prime}
is enough to provide some closeness of
\overline{B^{3,\varepsilon}_{t}},\overline{A^{3,\varepsilon}_{t}}
from being a solution of the 2D-3C model. The right choice for our purposes is
{{A}^{\varepsilon}_{t}}^{\prime}=K_{\varepsilon}[{{B}^{\varepsilon}_{t}}^{\prime}]
. The stochastic stretching term appearing in this case is not identically zero as for
\overline{A^{3,\varepsilon}_{t}}
, but can be treated as for (
2.38
).

Compared to
[
6
]
, the equations appearing for our system are more complicated since, even if linear, none of them is closed and they are interconnected in a not trivial way when computing a priori estimates. This is only partially due to the presence of the operator
\Lambda^{\varepsilon}_{\rho}
and the correlation between the components of the noise
W^{\varepsilon}_{t}
. We hope that this subsection could help the reader in order to understand the reasons behind the long computations of
section 3
and the heuristics for
Theorem 2.15
.

3.
A Priori Estimates

Recalling that

\displaystyle\overline{B}^{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}_{\mathcal{F}}(0,T;\mathbf{H}^{1}_{\varepsilon})\cap C_{\mathcal{F}}(0,T;\mathbf{L}^{2}_{\varepsilon})\quad\text{(resp. }{B^{\varepsilon}}^{\prime}\in L^{2}_{\mathcal{F}}(0,T;\mathbf{H}^{1}_{\varepsilon})\cap C_{\mathcal{F}}(0,T;\mathbf{L}^{2}_{\varepsilon})\text{),}

since
\overline{B}^{\varepsilon}
in independent on the third variable we have

\displaystyle\overline{B}^{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}_{\mathcal{F}}(0,T;\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{3}))\cap C_{\mathcal{F}}(0,T;\dot{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{3})).

The goal of
3.6
is to prove some bounds uniform in
\varepsilon>0
for quantities related to
\overline{B}^{\varepsilon}
in a common topology independent from
\varepsilon
. In order to do so, we start obtaining in
3.3
and
3.4
some pathwise estimates for quantities related to
\overline{B}^{\varepsilon}
and
{B^{\varepsilon}}^{\prime}
. Thanks to these we are able to gain in
3.5
some intermediate a priori bounds in topologies depending from
\varepsilon
. These results will imply
3.6
.

As discussed in
subsection 2.5
we will work with the potential fields associated to
\overline{B^{\varepsilon}_{t}}
and
{{B}^{\varepsilon}_{t}}^{\prime}
at a certain point. In order to do so we employ the following classical relations:

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
X\in\mathbf{L}^{2}_{\varepsilon},\ Y\in\mathbf{H}^{1}_{\varepsilon}
. Then

(3.1)

\displaystyle\left\|X\right\|^{2}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\left\|(-\Delta)^{-1/2}\operatorname{curl}X\right\|_{\varepsilon}^{2},\quad\left\|\nabla Y\right\|^{2}_{\varepsilon}=\left\|\operatorname{curl}Y\right\|_{\varepsilon}^{2}.

In particular if either
X
and
Y
are independent from the third variable, it holds

(3.2)

\displaystyle\left\|X^{3}\right\|^{2}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\left\|(-\Delta)^{-1/2}\nabla_{H}^{\perp}X^{3}\right\|_{\varepsilon}^{2},\quad\left\|\nabla Y^{3}\right\|^{2}_{\varepsilon}=\left\|\nabla_{H}^{\perp}Y^{3}\right\|_{\varepsilon}^{2}.

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
X:\mathbb{T}^{3}_{\varepsilon}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
a smooth, zero mean, divergence free vector field,
Y\in\mathbf{H}^{1}_{\varepsilon}
. Then

(3.3)

\displaystyle X\cdot\nabla Y-Y\cdot\nabla X\in\mathbf{L}^{2}_{\varepsilon}.

Moreover it holds

\displaystyle X\cdot\nabla Y-Y\cdot\nabla X

\displaystyle=\operatorname{curl}\left(X\cdot\nabla K_{\varepsilon}[Y]+(DX)K_{\varepsilon}[Y]\right)
